This is a security measure to prevent :ref:`HTTP Host header attacks\n    68→<host-headers-virtual-hosting>`, which are possible even under many\n    69→seemingly-safe web server configurations.\n    70→\n    71→Values in this list can be fully qualified names (e.g. ``'www.example.com'``),\n    72→in which case they will be matched against the request's ``Host`` header\n    73→exactly (case-insensitive, not including port). A value beginning with a period\n    74→can be used as a subdomain wildcard: ``'.example.com'`` will match\n    75→``example.com``, ``www.example.com``, and any other subdomain of\n    76→``example.com``. A value of ``'*'`` will match anything; in this case you are\n    77→responsible to provide your own validation of the ``Host`` header (perhaps in a\n    78→middleware; if so this middleware must be listed first in\n    79→:setting:`MIDDLEWARE`).\n    80→\n    81→Django also allows the `fully qualified domain name (FQDN)`_ of any entries.\n    82→Some browsers include a trailing dot in the ``Host`` header which Django\n    83→strips when performing host validation.\n    84→\n    85→.. _`fully qualified domain name (FQDN)`: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fully_qualified_domain_name\n    86→\n    87→If the ``Host`` header (or ``X-Forwarded-Host`` if\n    88→:setting:`USE_X_FORWARDED_HOST` is enabled) does not match any value in this\n    89→list, the :meth:`django.http.HttpRequest.get_host()` method will raise\n    90→:exc:`~django.core.exceptions.SuspiciousOperation`.\n    91→\n    92→When :setting:`DEBUG` is ``True`` and ``ALLOWED_HOSTS`` is empty, the host\n    93→is validated against ``['.localhost', '127.0.0.1', '[::1]']``.\n    94→\n    95→``ALLOWED_HOSTS`` is also :ref:`checked when running tests\n    96→<topics-testing-advanced-multiple-hosts>`.\n    97→\n    98→This validation only applies via :meth:`~django.http.HttpRequest.get_host()`;\n    99→if your code accesses the ``Host`` header directly from ``request.META`` you\n   100→are bypassing this security protection.\n   101→\n   102→.. setting:: APPEND_SLASH\n   103→\n   104→``APPEND_SLASH``\n   105→----------------\n   106→\n   107→Default: ``True``\n   108→\n   109→When set to ``True``, if the request URL does not match any of the patterns\n   110→in the URLconf and it doesn't end in a slash, an HTTP redirect is issued to the\n   111→same URL with a slash appended. Note that the redirect may cause any data\n   112→submitted in a POST request to be lost.\n   113→\n   114→The :setting:`APPEND_SLASH` setting is only used if\n   115→:class:`~django.middleware.common.CommonMiddleware` is installed\n   116→(see :doc:`/topics/http/middleware`). See also :setting:`PREPEND_WWW`.\n   117→\n   118→.. setting:: CACHES\n   119→\n   120→``CACHES``\n   121→----------\n   122→\n   123→Default::\n   124→\n   125→    {\n   126→        \"default\": {\n   127→            \"BACKEND\": \"django.core.cache.backends.locmem.LocMemCache\",\n   128→        }\n   129→    }\n   130→\n   131→A dictionary containing the settings for all caches to be used with\n   132→Django. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Pdtb3W122it2ee47vGR6Up","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→=============================\n     2→How to manage error reporting\n     3→=============================\n     4→\n     5→When you're running a public site you should always turn off the\n     6→:setting:`DEBUG` setting. That will make your server run much faster, and will\n     7→also prevent malicious users from seeing details of your application that can be\n     8→revealed by the error pages.\n     9→\n    10→However, running with :setting:`DEBUG` set to ``False`` means you'll never see\n    11→errors generated by your site -- everyone will instead see your public error\n    12→pages. You need to keep track of errors that occur in deployed sites, so Django\n    13→can be configured to create reports with details about those errors.\n    14→\n    15→Email reports\n    16→=============\n    17→\n    18→Server errors\n    19→-------------\n    20→\n    21→When :setting:`DEBUG` is ``False``, Django will email the users listed in the\n    22→:setting:`ADMINS` setting whenever your code raises an unhandled exception and\n    23→results in an internal server error (strictly speaking, for any response with\n    24→an HTTP status code of 500 or greater). This gives the administrators immediate\n    25→notification of any errors. The :setting:`ADMINS` will get a description of the\n    26→error, a complete Python traceback, and details about the HTTP request that\n    27→caused the error.\n    28→\n    29→.. note::\n    30→\n    31→   In order to send email, Django requires a few settings telling it\n    32→   how to connect to your mail server. At the very least, you'll need\n    33→   to specify :setting:`EMAIL_HOST` and possibly\n    34→   :setting:`EMAIL_HOST_USER` and :setting:`EMAIL_HOST_PASSWORD`,\n    35→   though other settings may be also required depending on your mail\n    36→   server's configuration. Consult :doc:`the Django settings\n    37→   documentation </ref/settings>` for a full list of email-related\n    38→   settings.\n    39→\n    40→By default, Django will send email from root@localhost. However, some mail\n    41→providers reject all email from this address. To use a different sender\n    42→address, modify the :setting:`SERVER_EMAIL` setting.\n    43→\n    44→To activate this behavior, put the email addresses of the recipients in the\n    45→:setting:`ADMINS` setting.\n    46→\n    47→.. seealso::\n    48→\n    49→    Server error emails are sent using the logging framework, so you can\n    50→    customize this behavior by :doc:`customizing your logging configuration\n    51→    </topics/logging>`.\n    52→\n    53→404 errors\n    54→----------\n    55→\n    56→Django can also be configured to email errors about broken links (404 \"page\n    57→not found\" errors). Django sends emails about 404 errors when:\n    58→\n    59→* :setting:`DEBUG` is ``False``;\n    60→\n    61→* Your :setting:`MIDDLEWARE` setting includes\n    62→  :class:`django.middleware.common.BrokenLinkEmailsMiddleware`.\n    63→\n    64→If those conditions are met, Django will email the users listed in the\n    65→:setting:`MANAGERS` setting whenever your code raises a 404 and the request has\n    66→a referer. It doesn't bother to email for 404s that don't have a referer --\n    67→those are usually people typing in broken URLs or broken web bots. It also\n    68→ignores 404s when the referer is equal to the requested URL, since this\n    69→behavior is from broken web bots too.\n    70→\n    71→.. note::\n    72→\n    73→    :class:`~django.middleware.common.BrokenLinkEmailsMiddleware` must appear\n    74→    before other middleware that intercepts 404 errors, such as\n    75→    :class:`~django.middleware.locale.LocaleMiddleware` or\n    76→    :class:`~django.contrib.flatpages.middleware.FlatpageFallbackMiddleware`.\n    77→    Put it toward the top of your :setting:`MIDDLEWARE` setting.\n    78→\n    79→You can tell Django to stop reporting particular 404s by tweaking the\n    80→:setting:`IGNORABLE_404_URLS` setting. It should be a list of compiled\n    81→regular expression objects. For example::\n    82→\n    83→    import re\n    84→\n    85→    IGNORABLE_404_URLS = [\n    86→        re.compile(r\"\\.(php|cgi)$\"),\n    87→        re.compile(r\"^/phpmyadmin/\"),\n    88→    ]\n    89→\n    90→In this example, a 404 to any URL ending with ``.php`` or ``.cgi`` will *not* be\n    91→reported. Neither will any URL starting with ``/phpmyadmin/``.\n    92→\n    93→The following example shows how to exclude some conventional URLs that browsers and\n    94→crawlers often request::\n    95→\n    96→    import re\n    97→\n    98→    IGNORABLE_404_URLS = [\n    99→        re.compile(r\"^/apple-touch-icon.*\\.png$\"),\n   100→        re.compile(r\"^/favicon\\.ico$\"),\n   101→        re.compile(r\"^/robots\\.txt$\"),\n   102→    ]\n   103→\n   104→(Note that these are regular expressions, so we put a backslash in front of\n   105→periods to escape them.)\n   106→\n   107→If you'd like to customize the behavior of\n   108→:class:`django.middleware.common.BrokenLinkEmailsMiddleware` further (for\n   109→example to ignore requests coming from web crawlers), you should subclass it\n   110→and override its methods.\n   111→\n   112→.. seealso::\n   113→\n   114→    404 errors are logged using the logging framework. By default, these log\n   115→    records are ignored, but you can use them for error reporting by writing a\n   116→    handler and :doc:`configuring logging </topics/logging>` appropriately.\n   117→\n   118→.. _filtering-error-reports:\n   119→\n   120→Filtering error reports\n   121→=======================\n   122→\n   123→.. warning::\n   124→\n   125→    Filtering sensitive data is a hard problem, and it's nearly impossible to\n   126→    guarantee that sensitive data won't leak into an error report. Therefore,\n   127→    error reports should only be available to trusted team members and you\n   128→    should avoid transmitting error reports unencrypted over the internet\n   129→    (such as through email).\n   130→\n   131→Filtering sensitive information\n   132→-------------------------------\n   133→\n   134→.. currentmodule:: django.views.decorators.debug\n   135→\n   136→Error reports are really helpful for debugging errors, so it is generally\n   137→useful to record as much relevant information about those errors as possible.\n   138→For example, by default Django records the `full traceback`_ for the\n   139→exception raised, each `traceback frame`_’s local variables, and the\n   140→:class:`~django.http.HttpRequest`’s :ref:`attributes<httprequest-attributes>`.\n   141→\n   142→However, sometimes certain types of information may be too sensitive and thus\n   143→may not be appropriate to be kept track of, for example a user's password or\n   144→credit card number. So in addition to filtering out settings that appear to be\n   145→sensitive as described in the :setting:`DEBUG` documentation, Django offers a\n   146→set of function decorators to help you control which information should be\n   147→filtered out of error reports in a production environment (that is, where\n   148→:setting:`DEBUG` is set to ``False``): :func:`sensitive_variables` and\n   149→:func:`sensitive_post_parameters`.\n   150→\n   151→.. _`full traceback`: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stack_trace\n   152→.. _`traceback frame`: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stack_frame\n   153→\n   154→.. function:: sensitive_variables(*variables)\n   155→\n   156→    If a function (either a view or any regular callback) in your code uses\n   157→    local variables susceptible to contain sensitive information, you may\n   158→    prevent the values of those variables from being included in error reports\n   159→    using the ``sensitive_variables`` decorator::\n   160→\n   161→        from django.views.decorators.debug import sensitive_variables\n   162→\n   163→\n   164→        @sensitive_variables(\"user\", \"pw\", \"cc\")\n   165→        def process_info(user):\n   166→            pw = user.pass_word\n   167→            cc = user.credit_card_number\n   168→            name = user.name\n   169→            ...\n   170→\n   171→    In the above example, the values for the ``user``, ``pw`` and ``cc``\n   172→    variables will be hidden and replaced with stars (``**********``)\n   173→    in the error reports, whereas the value of the ``name`` variable will be\n   174→    disclosed.\n   175→\n   176→    To systematically hide all local variables of a function from error logs,\n   177→    do not provide any argument to the ``sensitive_variables`` decorator::\n   178→\n   179→        @sensitive_variables()\n   180→        def my_function(): ...\n   181→\n   182→    .. admonition:: When using multiple decorators\n   183→\n   184→        If the variable you want to hide is also a function argument (e.g.\n   185→        '``user``’ in the following example), and if the decorated function has\n   186→        multiple decorators, then make sure to place ``@sensitive_variables``\n   187→        at the top of the decorator chain. This way it will also hide the\n   188→        function argument as it gets passed through the other decorators::\n   189→\n   190→            @sensitive_variables(\"user\", \"pw\", \"cc\")\n   191→            @some_decorator\n   192→            @another_decorator\n   193→            def process_info(user): ...\n   194→\n   195→.. function:: sensitive_post_parameters(*parameters)\n   196→\n   197→    If one of your views receives an :class:`~django.http.HttpRequest` object\n   198→    with :attr:`POST parameters<django.http.HttpRequest.POST>` susceptible to\n   199→    contain sensitive information, you may prevent the values of those\n   200→    parameters from being included in the error reports using the\n   201→    ``sensitive_post_parameters`` decorator::\n   202→\n   203→        from django.views.decorators.debug import sensitive_post_parameters\n   204→\n   205→\n   206→        @sensitive_post_parameters(\"pass_word\", \"credit_card_number\")\n   207→        def record_user_profile(request):\n   208→            UserProfile.create(\n   209→                user=request.user,\n   210→                password=request.POST[\"pass_word\"],\n   211→                credit_card=request.POST[\"credit_card_number\"],\n   212→                name=request.POST[\"name\"],\n   213→            )\n   214→            ...\n   215→\n   216→    In the above example, the values for the ``pass_word`` and\n   217→    ``credit_card_number`` POST parameters will be hidden and replaced with\n   218→    stars (``**********``) in the request's representation inside the\n   219→    error reports, whereas the value of the ``name`` parameter will be\n   220→    disclosed.\n   221→\n   222→    To systematically hide all POST parameters of a request in error reports,\n   223→    do not provide any argument to the ``sensitive_post_parameters`` decorator::\n   224→\n   225→        @sensitive_post_parameters()\n   226→        def my_view(request): ...\n   227→\n   228→    All POST parameters are systematically filtered out of error reports for\n   229→    certain :mod:`django.contrib.auth.views` views (``login``,\n   230→    ``password_reset_confirm``, ``password_change``, and ``add_view`` and\n   231→    ``user_change_password`` in the ``auth`` admin) to prevent the leaking of\n   232→    sensitive information such as user passwords.\n   233→\n   234→.. _custom-error-reports:\n   235→\n   236→Custom error reports\n   237→--------------------\n   238→\n   239→All :func:`sensitive_variables` and :func:`sensitive_post_parameters` do is,\n   240→respectively, annotate the decorated function with the names of sensitive\n   241→variables and annotate the ``HttpRequest`` object with the names of sensitive\n   242→POST parameters, so that this sensitive information can later be filtered out\n   243→of reports when an error occurs. The actual filtering is done by Django's\n   244→default error reporter filter:\n   245→:class:`django.views.debug.SafeExceptionReporterFilter`. This filter uses the\n   246→decorators' annotations to replace the corresponding values with stars\n   247→(``**********``) when the error reports are produced. If you wish to\n   248→override or customize this default behavior for your entire site, you need to\n   249→define your own filter class and tell Django to use it via the\n   250→:setting:`DEFAULT_EXCEPTION_REPORTER_FILTER` setting::\n   251→\n   252→    DEFAULT_EXCEPTION_REPORTER_FILTER = \"path.to.your.CustomExceptionReporterFilter\"\n   253→\n   254→You may also control in a more granular way which filter to use within any\n   255→given view by setting the ``HttpRequest``’s ``exception_reporter_filter``\n   256→attribute::\n   257→\n   258→    def my_view(request):\n   259→        if request.user.is_authenticated:\n   260→            request.exception_reporter_filter = CustomExceptionReporterFilter()\n   261→        ...\n   262→\n   263→.. currentmodule:: django.views.debug\n   264→\n   265→Your custom filter class needs to inherit from\n   266→:class:`django.views.debug.SafeExceptionReporterFilter` and may override the\n   267→following attributes and methods:\n   268→\n   269→.. class:: SafeExceptionReporterFilter\n   270→\n   271→    .. attribute:: cleansed_substitute\n   272→\n   273→        The string value to replace sensitive value with. By default it\n   274→        replaces the values of sensitive variables with stars\n   275→        (``**********``).\n   276→\n   277→    .. attribute:: hidden_settings\n   278→\n   279→        A compiled regular expression object used to match settings and\n   280→        ``request.META`` values considered as sensitive. By default equivalent\n   281→        to::\n   282→\n   283→            import re\n   284→\n   285→            re.compile(r\"API|AUTH|TOKEN|KEY|SECRET|PASS|SIGNATURE|HTTP_COOKIE\", flags=re.IGNORECASE)\n   286→\n   287→        .. versionchanged:: 5.2\n   288→\n   289→            The term ``AUTH`` was added.\n   290→\n   291→    .. method:: is_active(request)\n   292→\n   293→        Returns ``True`` to activate the filtering in\n   294→        :meth:`get_post_parameters` and :meth:`get_traceback_frame_variables`.\n   295→        By default the filter is active if :setting:`DEBUG` is ``False``. Note\n   296→        that sensitive ``request.META`` values are always filtered along with\n   297→        sensitive setting values, as described in the :setting:`DEBUG`\n   298→        documentation.\n   299→\n   300→    .. method:: get_post_parameters(request)\n   301→\n   302→        Returns the filtered dictionary of POST parameters. Sensitive values\n   303→        are replaced with :attr:`cleansed_substitute`.\n   304→\n   305→    .. method:: get_traceback_frame_variables(request, tb_frame)\n   306→\n   307→        Returns the filtered dictionary of local variables for the given\n   308→        traceback frame. Sensitive values are replaced with\n   309→        :attr:`cleansed_substitute`.\n   310→\n   311→If you need to customize error reports beyond filtering you may specify a\n   312→custom error reporter class by defining the\n   313→:setting:`DEFAULT_EXCEPTION_REPORTER` setting::\n   314→\n   315→    DEFAULT_EXCEPTION_REPORTER = \"path.to.your.CustomExceptionReporter\"\n   316→\n   317→The exception reporter is responsible for compiling the exception report data,\n   318→and formatting it as text or HTML appropriately. (The exception reporter uses\n   319→:setting:`DEFAULT_EXCEPTION_REPORTER_FILTER` when preparing the exception\n   320→report data.)\n   321→\n   322→Your custom reporter class needs to inherit from\n   323→:class:`django.views.debug.ExceptionReporter`.\n   324→\n   325→.. class:: ExceptionReporter\n   326→\n   327→    .. attribute:: html_template_path\n   328→\n   329→        Property that returns a :class:`pathlib.Path` representing the absolute\n   330→        filesystem path to a template for rendering the HTML representation of\n   331→        the exception. Defaults to the Django provided template.\n   332→\n   333→    .. attribute:: text_template_path\n   334→\n   335→        Property that returns a :class:`pathlib.Path` representing the absolute\n   336→        filesystem path to a template for rendering the plain-text\n   337→        representation of the exception. Defaults to the Django provided\n   338→        template.\n   339→\n   340→    .. method:: get_traceback_data()\n   341→\n   342→        Return a dictionary containing traceback information.\n   343→\n   344→        This is the main extension point for customizing exception reports, for\n   345→        example::\n   346→\n   347→            from django.views.debug import ExceptionReporter\n   348→\n   349→\n   350→            class CustomExceptionReporter(ExceptionReporter):\n   351→                def get_traceback_data(self):\n   352→                    data = super().get_traceback_data()\n   353→                    # ... remove/add something here ...\n   354→                    return data\n   355→\n   356→    .. method:: get_traceback_html()\n   357→\n   358→        Return HTML version of exception report.\n   359→\n   360→        Used for HTML version of debug 500 HTTP error page.\n   361→\n   362→    .. method:: get_traceback_text()\n   363→\n   364→        Return plain text version of exception report.\n   365→\n   366→        Used for plain text version of debug 500 HTTP error page and email\n   367→        reports.\n   368→\n   369→As with the filter class, you may control which exception reporter class to use\n   370→within any given view by setting the ``HttpRequest``’s\n   371→``exception_reporter_class`` attribute::\n   372→\n   373→    def my_view(request):\n   374→        if request.user.is_authenticated:\n   375→            request.exception_reporter_class = CustomExceptionReporter()\n   376→        ...\n   377→\n   378→.. seealso::\n   379→\n   380→    You can also set up custom error reporting by writing a custom piece of\n   381→    :ref:`exception middleware <exception-middleware>`. If you do write custom\n   382→    error handling, it's a good idea to emulate Django's built-in error handling\n   383→    and only report/log errors if :setting:`DEBUG` is ``False``.\n   384→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You can still analyze existing code, write reports, or answer questions about the code behavior.\n</system-reminder>\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_01JJVLT6Yf5qbv1dvYdDrnDu","session_id":"d1fa49a5-5ecc-4430-bf57-e03b72e0edbd","uuid":"2c0f9d60-dc10-40b1-b918-139bbb0592ae"}

Migrating to a plain `[email, ...]` string list requires changes to: the validation logic and email-extraction logic in `django/core/mail/__init__.py`, the format comment in `django/conf/global_settings.py`, two documentation files that show the tuple example, and all test fixtures that supply the old format.\n\n## Root Cause\n\nThe core consumer of both settings is the private function `_send_server_message()` in `django/core/mail/__init__.py` (lines 122–147). It:\n\n1. **Reads** the setting via `getattr(settings, setting_name)` (line 131).\n2. **Validates** that every element is a list or tuple with exactly 2 items (line 135):\n   ```python\n   if not all(isinstance(a, (list, tuple)) and len(a) == 2 for a in recipients):\n       raise ValueError(f\"The {setting_name} setting must be a list of 2-tuples.\")\n   ```\n3. **Extracts** the email address by taking index `[1]` from each tuple (line 142):\n   ```python\n   to=[a[1] for a in recipients],\n   ```\n\nAll higher-level callers (`mail_admins`, `mail_managers`, `AdminEmailHandler`, `BrokenLinkEmailsMiddleware`, `sendtestemail`) reach `_send_server_message` transitively and therefore inherit the tuple dependency.\n\n## Evidence\n\n### Source Files\n\n| File | Line(s) | Role | Key Code Excerpt — Validation and Extraction\n\n```python\n# django/core/mail/__init__.py, lines 135–142\nif not all(isinstance(a, (list, tuple)) and len(a) == 2 for a in recipients):\n    raise ValueError(f\"The {setting_name} setting must be a list of 2-tuples.\")\n\nmail = EmailMultiAlternatives(\n    ...\n    to=[a[1] for a in recipients],   # extracts index-1 (email) from each 2-tuple\n    ...\n)\n```\n\n## Affected Components\n\n1. **`django/core/mail/`** — Primary consumer; `_send_server_message`, `mail_admins`, `mail_managers` all depend on tuple format.\n2. **`django/conf/global_settings.py`** — Default definition comment documents old format.\n3. **`django/utils/log.py`** — `AdminEmailHandler` transitively depends via `mail_admins()`.\n4. **`django/middleware/common.py`** — `BrokenLinkEmailsMiddleware` transitively depends via `mail_managers()`.\n5. **`django/core/management/commands/sendtestemail.py`** — Management command (no direct format dependency, but calls mail_admins/mail_managers).\n6. **`docs/ref/settings.txt`** — Canonical format specification shows tuple examples.\n7. **`docs/internals/contributing/writing-documentation.txt`** — Duplicate tuple example used as RST markup tutorial.\n8. **`tests/mail/tests.py`** — Tests exercise the tuple format and assert the \"list of 2-tuples\" error message.\n9. **`tests/mail/test_sendtestemail.py`** — Class fixture uses old tuple format.\n10. **`tests/logging_tests/tests.py`** — Multiple test methods use old tuple format.\n11. **`tests/middleware/tests.py`** — `BrokenLinkEmailsMiddleware` test uses old tuple format.\n12. **`tests/view_tests/tests/test_debug.py`** — Debug email tests use old tuple format.\n\n## Recommendation\n\n### Migration Checklist\n\n#### 1. `django/core/mail/__init__.py` — **Critical**\n- **Lines 135–136**: Replace the `isinstance(a, (list, tuple)) and len(a) == 2` validation with a check that each element `a` is a plain string (e.g., `isinstance(a, str)`).\n- **Line 136**: Update the error message from `\"must be a list of 2-tuples\"` to reflect the new expected format.\n- **Line 142**: Replace `to=[a[1] for a in recipients]` with `to=list(recipients)` (or equivalent), since each element will now be the email address directly.\n\n#### 2. `django/conf/global_settings.py` — **Low risk**\n- **Lines 24–25**: Update the comment from `# [('Full Name', 'email@example.com'), ...]` to `# ['email@example.com', ...]` (or remove the name component).\n\n#### 3. `docs/ref/settings.txt` — **Documentation**\n- **Lines 55–57**: Replace the format description (\"a tuple of (Full name, email address)\") and the example (`[(\"John\", \"john@example.com\"), ...]`) with the new string-list format.\n- **Lines 2066–2075**: MANAGERS description defers to ADMINS, so the ADMINS update covers it; verify the wording still reads correctly.\n\n#### 4. `docs/internals/contributing/writing-documentation.txt` — **Documentation**\n- **Lines 609–614**: Update the RST example for ADMINS (used as a documentation tutorial) to show the new format.\n\n#### 5. `tests/mail/tests.py` — **Tests**\n- **Lines 1780–1803** (`test_mail_admins_and_managers`): Replace all tuple test values with plain email strings; update `for _, address in value` unpacking to `for address in value`.\n- **Lines 1862–1884** (`test_wrong_admins_managers`): Rewrite to test *new* invalid inputs (e.g., a list of tuples, non-string elements). Update the hardcoded error message string (`\"list of 2-tuples\"`) to match whatever the new error message says.\n- **Lines 1140–1165**: Replace `[(\"nobody\", \"nobody@example.com\")]` fixtures with `[\"nobody@example.com\"]`.\n- **Lines 1805, 1820, 1836–1837**: Replace `[(\"nobody\", ...)]` fixtures with plain string lists.\n\n#### 6. `tests/mail/test_sendtestemail.py` — **Tests**\n- **Lines 6–15**: Replace the class-level `@override_settings` fixture from tuple format to string list. Update recipient assertions (lines 70–75, 86–91, 102–115) which currently assert on specific email addresses extracted from the tuple — the addresses themselves stay the same, so recipient assertions should still pass once the setting format is updated.\n\n#### 7. `tests/logging_tests/tests.py` — **Tests**\n- **Lines 251, 283, 322, 344, 375, 396, 438, 452, 570**: Replace all `[(\"admin name\", \"admin@example.com\")]` fixtures with `[\"admin@example.com\"]`. Migrating to a plain `[email, ...]` string list requires changes to: the validation logic and email-extraction logic in `django/core/mail/__init__.py`, the format comment in `django/conf/global_settings.py`, two documentation files that show the tuple example, and all test fixtures that supply the old format.\n\n## Root Cause\n\nThe core consumer of both settings is the private function `_send_server_message()` in `django/core/mail/__init__.py` (lines 122–147). It:\n\n1. **Reads** the setting via `getattr(settings, setting_name)` (line 131).\n2. **Validates** that every element is a list or tuple with exactly 2 items (line 135):\n   ```python\n   if not all(isinstance(a, (list, tuple)) and len(a) == 2 for a in recipients):\n       raise ValueError(f\"The {setting_name} setting must be a list of 2-tuples.\")\n   ```\n3. **Extracts** the email address by taking index `[1]` from each tuple (line 142):\n   ```python\n   to=[a[1] for a in recipients],\n   ```\n\nAll higher-level callers (`mail_admins`, `mail_managers`, `AdminEmailHandler`, `BrokenLinkEmailsMiddleware`, `sendtestemail`) reach `_send_server_message` transitively and therefore inherit the tuple dependency.\n\n## Evidence\n\n### Source Files\n\n| File | Line(s) | Role | check that each element `a` is a plain string (e.g., `isinstance(a, str)`).\n- **Line 136**: Update the error message from `\"must be a list of 2-tuples\"` to reflect the new expected format.\n- **Line 142**: Replace `to=[a[1] for a in recipients]` with `to=list(recipients)` (or equivalent), since each element will now be the email address directly.\n\n#### 2. `django/conf/global_settings.py` 5. `tests/mail/tests.py` — **Tests**\n- **Lines 1780–1803** (`test_mail_admins_and_managers`): Replace all tuple test values with plain email strings; update `for _, address in value` unpacking to `for address in value`.\n- **Lines 1862–1884** (`test_wrong_admins_managers`): Rewrite to test *new* invalid inputs (e.g., a list of tuples, non-string elements).
